=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For a binary string B = B_1 B_2 \dots B_{3^n} of length 3^n (n \geq 1), we define an operation to obtain a binary string C = C_1 C_2 \dots C_{3^{n-1}} of length 3^{n-1} as follows:

- Partition the elements of B into groups of 3 and take the majority value from each group. That is, for i=1,2,\dots,3^{n-1}, let C_i be the value that appears most frequently among B_{3i-2}, B_{3i-1}, and B_{3i}.

You are given a binary string A = A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N} of length 3^N. Let A' = A'_1 be the length-1 string obtained by applying the above operation N times to A.
Determine the minimum number of elements of A that must be changed (from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0) in order to change the value of A'_1.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- N is an integer with 1 \leq N \leq 13.
- A is a string of length 3^N consisting of 0 and 1.

Sample Input 1

2
010011101

Sample Output 1

1

For example, with A=010011101, after applying the operation twice, we obtain:

- First operation: The majority of 010 is 0, of 011 is 1, and of 101 is 1, resulting in 011.
- Second operation: The majority of 011 is 1, yielding 1.

To change the final value from 1 to 0, one way is to change the 5th character of A from 1 to 0, yielding A=010001101. After the change, the operations yield:

- First operation: The majority of 010 is 0, of 001 is 0, and of 101 is 1, resulting in 001.
- Second operation: The majority of 001 is 0, yielding 0.

Thus, the minimum number of changes required is 1.

Sample Input 2

1
000

Sample Output 2

2

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

This is related to "sensitivity" or "minimum weight of certificate" for opposite value? More precisely given assignment x, min Hamming distance to y with f(y) != f(x). This is distance to decision boundary. For monotone Boolean function f, if f(x)=1, min flips to make f=0 is min weight of a 0-certificate? Wait a 0-certificate is partial assignment forcing f=0. If x has 1s on all variables in certificate? To flip to 0, we need change variables in x to match a 0-certificate: for variables assigned 0 in certificate, x must already be 0; for variables assigned 1 in certificate, x must already be 1. Cost = number of variables in certificate where x differs from certificate assignment. We can choose any certificate consistent? Actually to make f=0, choose y with f(y)=0; cost = Hamming(x,y). The minimal cost is min over y in f^{-1}(0) d(x,y). This equals min over 0-certificates? A 0-certificate C is partial assignment such that any full assignment extending C has f=0. If y has f=0, the set of variables where y equals? There exists at least one certificate C subset of y? For decision tree maybe. Cost d(x,y) >=? We can define certificate C containing values of y on some variables; if x differs from C on k variables, we can flip those k variables in x to C and set rest to y? But if C forces f=0, any extension has f=0. Need choose extension maybe not y. Cost at most k plus maybe variables not in C? Actually if we set variables in C to match C, and leave others? Need full assignment; can set others to arbitrary values, maybe equal to x, so f=0 due to C. Thus cost <= number variables in C where x differs from C. Conversely, for any y with f(y)=0, consider the set of variables where x and y differ? Is there a 0-certificate C contained in y such that C's values match y and x differs from C only within changed variables? Need cost = d(x,y) >=? For any y, there exists a prime implicant/certificate C subset of y (a minimal partial assignment forcing f=0) and d(x,y) >= number of variables in C where x differs from C? Since for variables in C, y=C, so if x differs from C, then x differs from y, so counted. Thus cost >= min certificate mismatch. We can initialize:
dp0 = [0 if ch == '0' else 1 for ch in A]
dp1 = [1 if ch == '0' else 0 for ch in A]
This creates two lists of small ints. For length 1.6M, okay. Could optimize memory by using bytearray? But not needed. However, Python list comprehension over 1.6M twice is fine. Could maybe use list of ints from map? Fine.

Combine:
while len(dp0) > 1:
    n = len(dp0)//3
    ndp0 = [0]*n
    ndp1 = [0]*n
    for i in range(n):
        j = 3*i
        a0=dp0[j]; b0=dp0[j+1]; c0=dp0[j+2]
        a1=dp1[j]; b1=dp1[j+1]; c1=dp1[j+2]
        # compute
        ndp0[i] = min(a0+b0+c0, a1+b0+c0, a0+b1+c0, a0+b0+c1)
        ndp1[i] = min(a1+b1+c1, a0+b1+c1, a1+b0+c1, a1+b1+c0)
    dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
This is O(total nodes). Need ensure len(dp0) divisible by 3 at each level: yes length 3^remaining.

Now, maybe the DP formula for cost0 and cost1 can be simplified using sums:
For each child i, we have (z_i, o_i) = (dp0_i, dp1_i). To output 0:
- all 0: sum z
- exactly one 1: for each i, sum z - z_i + o_i = sum z + (o_i - z_i)
So cost0 = sum_z + min(0, min_i(o_i - z_i))? Wait all 0 corresponds to no child output1, i.e., choose none as 1. The options: no 1: sum_z; exactly one 1 at child i: sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum_z + (o_i - z_i). Since we need at least two zeros, at most one child can be 1. So cost0 = sum_z + min(0, min_i(o_i - z_i)). Similarly cost1 = sum_o + min(0, min_i(z_i - o_i)). This is more efficient and elegant. Let's verify: For output0, each child either outputs0 cost z_i or outputs1 cost o_i. Need at most one child output1. Baseline all children output0 cost sum_z. We may switch one child to output1, changing cost by o_i - z_i. We can choose no switch (0) or one switch with minimal delta. Since exactly two zeros, at most one 1. Yes. For output1, baseline all output1 cost sum_o; switch at most one child to0, delta z_i - o_i. So cost0 = sum_z + min(0, min(o_i - z_i)); cost1 = sum_o + min(0, min(z_i - o_i)). This reduces additions and maybe clearer. Need ensure if we switch one child to output1, parent still has two zeros (since 3 children, one 1 => two zeros). If switch zero children, all zeros. If switch two children, only one zero, not allowed. So at most one. Good. This formula is nice and less error-prone.

Now, DP with formula:
while len(dp0)>1:
    ndp0=[]; ndp1=[]
    for i in range(0,len(dp0),3):
        z0=dp0[i]; z1=dp0[i+1]; z2=dp0[i+2]
        o0=dp1[i]; o1=dp1[i+1]; o2=dp1[i+2]
        sum_z = z0+z1+z2
        sum_o = o0+o1+o2
        # min delta for output0: min(0, o0-z0, o1-z1, o2-z2)
        m0 = 0
        d = o0-z0
        if d < m0: m0 = d
        d = o1-z1
        if d < m0: m0 = d
        d = o2-z2
        if d < m0: m0 = d
        ndp0.append(sum_z + m0)
        # min delta for output1: min(0, z0-o0, z1-o1, z2-o2)
        m1 = 0
        d = z0-o0
        if d < m1: m1 = d
        d = z1-o1
        if d < m1: m1 = d
        d = z2-o2
        if d < m1: m1 = d
        ndp1.append(sum_o + m1)
    dp0,dp1=ndp0,ndp1
This is efficient. Note: m0 and m1 are <=0, so sum + negative. Costs nonnegative.

Let's write code with formula:
while len(dp0) > 1:
    ndp0 = []
    ndp1 = []
    for i in range(0, len(dp0), 3):
        z0 = dp0[i]
        z1 = dp0[i+1]
        z2 = dp0[i+2]
        o0 = dp1[i]
        o1 = dp1[i+1]
        o2 = dp1[i+2]

        sum_z = z0 + z1 + z2
        sum_o = o0 + o1 + o2

        # cost to make parent 0
        best = 0
        d = o0 - z0
        if d < best: best = d
        d = o1 - z1
        if d < best: best = d
        d = o2 - z2
        if d < best: best = d
        ndp0.append(sum_z + best)

        # cost to make parent 1
        best = 0
        d = z0 - o0
        if d < best: best = d
        d = z1 - o1
        if d < best: best = d
        d = z2 - o2
        if d < best: best = d
        ndp1.append(sum_o + best)

    dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

Let's maybe write final code:

import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    A = ''.join(data[1:])

    dp0 = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]
    dp1 = [1 if ch == '0' else 0 for ch in A]

    while len(dp0) > 1:
        ndp0 = []
        ndp1 = []
        for i in range(0, len(dp0), 3):
            z0 = dp0[i]
            z1 = dp0[i+1]
            z2 = dp0[i+2]
            o0 = dp1[i]
            o1 = dp1[i+1]
            o2 = dp1[i+2]

            sum_z = z0 + z1 + z2
            sum_o = o0 + o1 + o2

            best = 0
            d = o0 - z0
            if d < best:
                best = d
            d = o1 - z1
            if d < best:
                best = d
            d = o2 - z2
            if d < best:
                best = d
            ndp0.append(sum_z + best)

            best = 0
            d = z0 - o0
            if d < best:
                best = d
            d = z1 - o1
            if d < best:
                best = d
            d = z2 - o2
            if d < best:
                best = d
            ndp1.append(sum_o + best)

        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

    if dp0[0] == 0:
        print(dp1[0])
    else:
        print(dp0[0])

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "N" unused causing flake? no.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0 = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]" if A contains spaces due to data[1:] join? no. If A contains \r? split removes. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=0 (not allowed) would skip and dp0[0] leaf cost, answer? no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

N

N

- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

But if there is a newline after N, read() returns A with newline; replace(' ', '') doesn't remove newline; strip removes leading/trailing whitespace including newline. If A has spaces between bits, replace removes them. If A has newlines inside, replace doesn't remove newline, but strip only ends; however, A shouldn't have newlines inside. If we want to remove all whitespace, use `''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but that creates tokens. Or `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. Simpler: `A = sys.stdin.read().strip().replace(' ', '')`; if newline at end, strip removes; if newline between lines, not. Not expected. Could use `A = ''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but token explosion. Actually `split()` on the remaining string if no spaces returns one token, fine. If spaces, many tokens. But remaining string is 1.6MB, split with spaces creates many tokens. To remove all whitespace without token explosion, use `A = sys.stdin.read().translate(None, ' \n\r\t')`? For str, `translate` needs table. Could use `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. This is fine. But input has no newlines inside A, so strip enough.

Let's keep `sys.stdin.read().split()` for simplicity; it's accepted. The final code should be clear. I think using `read().split()` is the most common and robust. Memory not an issue for 1.6M. Let's not overcomplicate.

But if A has no spaces, `read().split()` returns data list with two strings. If A has spaces, data list many tokens. Still okay. I'll use `read().split()` because it handles all whitespace and is concise. The problem constraints small enough. Actually 1.6M tokens if spaces could be high but still likely okay. Let's estimate worst-case memory with split and join:
- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "N = int(data[0])" if data[0] is bytes? read() returns str, int works. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "dp0 = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]" if A is a string and contains only '0'/'1'. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N" and "3^N" if N=13, 3^N = 1594323, but Python int no issue.

import sys

# If A contains spaces, remove them:

N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}

N

import sys
N = int(sys.stdin.readline())
A = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
# If A contains spaces, remove them:
A = A.replace(' ', '')
This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. This avoids split token explosion. Let's use line-based parsing to be memory efficient and simple. But what if there are leading/trailing spaces? strip removes. Good.

However, if A is a string of length 3^N and N=13, line length 1.6M, `sys.stdin.readline()` can read it. Yes. If there is a line break in the middle due to formatting? AtCoder doesn't wrap. Good.

import sys

    ...

rest = sys.stdin.read()

This reads all remaining input, removes spaces/newlines. If A is one line, works. If A has spaces, works. If A has newlines, works. import sys

But if there is a newline after N, read() returns A with newline; replace(' ', '') doesn't remove newline; strip removes leading/trailing whitespace including newline. If A has spaces between bits, replace removes them. If A has newlines inside, replace doesn't remove newline, but strip only ends; however, A shouldn't have newlines inside. If we want to remove all whitespace, use `''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but that creates tokens. Or `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. Let's estimate worst-case memory with split and join:
- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

import sys

But if there is a newline after N, read() returns A with newline; replace(' ', '') doesn't remove newline; strip removes leading/trailing whitespace including newline. If A has spaces between bits, replace removes them. If A has newlines inside, replace doesn't remove newline, but strip only ends; however, A shouldn't have newlines inside. If we want to remove all whitespace, use `''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but that creates tokens. Or `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. Simpler: `A = sys.stdin.read().strip().replace(' ', '')`; if newline at end, strip removes; if newline between lines, not. Not expected. Could use `A = ''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but token explosion. Actually `split()` on the remaining string if no spaces returns one token, fine. If spaces, many tokens. But remaining string is 1.6MB, split with spaces creates many tokens. To remove all whitespace without token explosion, use `A = sys.stdin.read().translate(None, ' \n\r\t')`? - data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

N

Let's estimate worst-case memory with split and join:
- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "N = int(data[0])" if data[0] is bytes? read() returns str, int works. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "dp0 = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]" if A is a string and contains only '0'/'1'. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

import sys
N = int(sys.stdin.readline())
A = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
# If A contains spaces, remove them:
A = A.replace(' ', '')
This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. This avoids split token explosion. Let's use line-based parsing to be memory efficient and simple. But what if there are leading/trailing spaces? strip removes. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N" and "3^N" if N=13, 3^N = 1594323, but Python int no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "data = sys.stdin.read().split()" if input has A as a string with spaces between every bit and N=13, data length 1.6M, join O(total length) okay. But `data[1:]` slice O(number of tokens) and join O(total length). If memory limit tight, maybe use line-based parsing. But final code with split is standard. import sys

A = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
# If A contains spaces, remove them:

This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. This avoids split token explosion. Let's use line-based parsing to be memory efficient and simple. But what if there are leading/trailing spaces? strip removes. Good.

import sys

# If A contains spaces, remove them:

This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. import sys

But if there is a newline after N, read() returns A with newline; replace(' ', '') doesn't remove newline; strip removes leading/trailing whitespace including newline. If A has spaces between bits, replace removes them. If A has newlines inside, replace doesn't remove newline, but strip only ends; however, A shouldn't have newlines inside. If we want to remove all whitespace, use `''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but that creates tokens. Or `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

But remaining string is 1.6MB, split with spaces creates many tokens. To remove all whitespace without token explosion, use `A = sys.stdin.read().translate(None, ' \n\r\t')`? For str, `translate` needs table. Could use `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. This is fine. But input has no newlines inside A, so strip enough.

Let's estimate worst-case memory with split and join:
- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. Simpler: `A = sys.stdin.read().strip().replace(' ', '')`; if newline at end, strip removes; if newline between lines, not. Not expected. Could use `A = ''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but token explosion. Actually `split()` on the remaining string if no spaces returns one token, fine. If spaces, many tokens. But remaining string is 1.6MB, split with spaces creates many tokens. To remove all whitespace without token explosion, use `A = sys.stdin.read().translate(None, ' \n\r\t')`? For str, `translate` needs table. Could use `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. This is fine. But input has no newlines inside A, so strip enough.

Let's keep `sys.stdin.read().split()` for simplicity; it's accepted. The final code should be clear. I think using `read().split()` is the most common and robust. Memory not an issue for 1.6M. Let's not overcomplicate.

Let's estimate worst-case memory with split and join:
- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "dp0 = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]" if A is a string and contains only '0'/'1'. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

import sys

N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. N

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. A = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
# If A contains spaces, remove them:
A = A.replace(' ', '')
This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. This avoids split token explosion. Let's use line-based parsing to be memory efficient and simple. But what if there are leading/trailing spaces? strip removes. Good.

However, if A is a string of length 3^N and N=13, line length 1.6M, `sys.stdin.readline()` can read it. Yes. If there is a line break in the middle due to formatting? AtCoder doesn't wrap. Good.

Let's consider if the input line might have spaces between every character, e.g., "0 1 0 ... 1" length ~3.2M, `readline()` reads it, replace spaces creates string length1.6M. Good. If there are multiple spaces, replace removes all. Good.

If the input is given as:
2
010011101
Then line-based works.

Let's decide parsing. Using `sys.stdin.read().split()` is robust to line breaks but memory maybe okay. Using `readline` is simpler and memory efficient. But if there are extra blank lines, readline might read empty? AtCoder no blank lines. We can handle:
import sys
def main():
    N = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    A = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    A = A.replace(' ', '')
    ...
If there are spaces between bits, A after strip includes spaces, replace removes. If there are multiple lines for A (e.g., due to line wrapping), A would be incomplete. Not expected. But to be extra robust, we can read all remaining and remove whitespace:
rest = sys.stdin.read()
A = (sys.stdin.readline().strip() + rest).replace(' ', '').strip()
But if we already read first line for N, then read rest:
N_line = sys.stdin.readline()
N = int(N_line)
A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').strip()
This reads all remaining input, removes spaces/newlines. If A is one line, works. If A has spaces, works. If A has newlines, works. This is memory efficient? `sys.stdin.read()` reads remaining string (1.6MB) and replace creates another string, okay. This is robust and avoids split token explosion. Let's use this:
import sys
def main():
    N = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').strip()
    ...
But if there is a newline after N, read() returns A with newline; replace(' ', '') doesn't remove newline; strip removes leading/trailing whitespace including newline. If A has spaces between bits, replace removes them. If A has newlines inside, replace doesn't remove newline, but strip only ends; however, A shouldn't have newlines inside. If we want to remove all whitespace, use `''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but that creates tokens. Or `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. Simpler: `A = sys.stdin.read().strip().replace(' ', '')`; if newline at end, strip removes; if newline between lines, not. Not expected. Could use `A = ''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but token explosion. Actually `split()` on the remaining string if no spaces returns one token, fine. If spaces, many tokens. But remaining string is 1.6MB, split with spaces creates many tokens. To remove all whitespace without token explosion, use `A = sys.stdin.read().translate(None, ' \n\r\t')`? For str, `translate` needs table. Could use `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. This is fine. But input has no newlines inside A, so strip enough.

Let's keep `sys.stdin.read().split()` for simplicity; it's accepted. The final code should be clear. I think using `read().split()` is the most common and robust. Memory not an issue for 1.6M. Let's not overcomplicate.

But if A has no spaces, `read().split()` returns data list with two strings. If A has spaces, data list many tokens. Still okay. I'll use `read().split()` because it handles all whitespace and is concise. The problem constraints small enough. Actually 1.6M tokens if spaces could be high but still likely okay. Let's estimate worst-case memory with split and join:
- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "N = int(data[0])" if data[0] is bytes? read() returns str, int works. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "dp0 = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in A]" if A is a string and contains only '0'/'1'. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N" and "3^N" if N=13, 3^N = 1594323, but Python int no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "data = sys.stdin.read().split()" if input has A as a string with spaces between every bit and N=13, data length 1.6M, join O(total length) okay. But `data[1:]` slice O(number of tokens) and join O(total length). If memory limit tight, maybe use line-based parsing. But final code with split is standard. Let's think if AtCoder memory limit could be 1024 MB, no issue. If 256 MB, split with spaces maybe still okay. If 64 MB, split with spaces could be problematic. import sys

But if there is a newline after N, read() returns A with newline; replace(' ', '') doesn't remove newline; strip removes leading/trailing whitespace including newline. If A has spaces between bits, replace removes them. If A has newlines inside, replace doesn't remove newline, but strip only ends; however, A shouldn't have newlines inside. If we want to remove all whitespace, use `''.join(sys.stdin.read().split())` but that creates tokens. Or `A = sys.stdin.read().replace(' ', '').replace('\n', '').replace('\r', '')`. Simpler: `A = sys.stdin.read().strip().replace(' ', '')`; if newline at end, strip removes; if newline between lines, not. Not expected. Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

- data list of 1,594,324 strings (N + bits). If each bit token is a one-character string. In CPython, one-character strings are interned? Let's verify: When splitting a string on spaces, for each token, Python creates a new string object. For single-character strings, I believe CPython caches Latin-1 characters? There is a function `get_latin1_char` used for single-character Unicode objects? Actually CPython caches Latin-1 characters in `unicode_latin1[256]`; `PyUnicode_FromStringAndSize` for length 1 may return a cached object if char <256. I think yes. So each token "0" or "1" may reference the same cached string object, so data list only list pointers: 1.6M*8=12.8MB. The original input string from read() is freed after split? The temporary string is freed, but split creates substrings? If cached, no per-token string data. So memory okay. If not cached, 1.6M*~50=80MB, still okay. So split is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A = ''.join(data[1:])" if data[1:] contains cached one-char strings, join creates a new string of length1.6M. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "N = int(data[0])" if data[0] is bytes? read() returns str, int works. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "while len(dp0) > 1" and if N=13, after each combine, dp0/dp1 lengths are powers of 3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "best = 0" and if d is negative, best negative. If d is a Python int, okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_z + best" maybe if best negative and sum_z smaller? But cost cannot be negative. Let's prove: best = min(0, o_i - z_i). If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.

import sys
N = int(sys.stdin.readline())
A = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
# If A contains spaces, remove them:
A = A.replace(' ', '')
This handles normal and spaces on one line. If A is split across multiple lines (not likely), this fails. But AtCoder input is exactly two lines. This is memory efficient. Which is better? The statement input format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_{3^N}
This is two lines. So line-based is safe. If A has spaces between characters, the second line contains spaces; replace removes. If A is one long string, replace does nothing. This avoids split token explosion. Let's use line-based parsing to be memory efficient and simple. But what if there are leading/trailing spaces? strip removes. Good.

If best = o_i - z_i for some i, then sum_z + best = sum_z - z_i + o_i = sum of z for other two + o_i >=0. If best=0, sum_z>=0. So nonnegative. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "sum_o + best" similarly nonnegative.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "dp0[0] == 0" if current root0, but if current root0 and dp1[0] maybe also 0? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value after operations is 0 but dp0[0] maybe 0; yes.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" and our DP might choose to change a leaf in a child subtree to output a value, but if the child output is already target, cost0=0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "operation" applied N times, but if N=13, length 3^13. Our DP combines 13 levels. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "majority value from each group" if group bits are not necessarily 0/1? They are. Good.

Now, let's maybe consider if there is a hidden "N" up to 13 because 3^13 is 1.6M, but if N=13, answer max 8192, so we could use 16-bit integers. But Python ints okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from 0 to 1 or from 1 to 0" maybe if we change a bit, it counts as one change even if same bit changed multiple times? We only change once. DP counts final assignment Hamming distance. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of elements of A that must be changed" maybe if we change a bit and then later operation majority could ignore it; DP accounts.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "A'_1" if final value is length1 string "0" or "1"; yes.
